## Seam 3 — `flightcrew-buildout` (+38 from main, to 2026-09-15)

**Attempted.** The all-in-one build. Per `bab64608`: "an end-to-end orchestration system for flightdeck fitted into flightcrew/, launch/, testbench/ and manuals/: the fc runner and command modules, check libraries with validators and gates, ten crew roles, six hooks with a settings fragment, schemas, kickoff library and dispatch templates, three dynamic workflow scripts, manuals, and a 30-suite testbench." Method: "spec frozen first, 29 locked suites derived before implementation, contracts settled by two refutation rounds, seven Opus units behind deterministic Sonnet gates, integration to green, four sealed-room Fable critics with adjudication". Outcome: "testbench 30 of 30 suites and 527 cases with hygiene clean". It ran two launches of itself, then merged PR #1 from `flightcrew-buildout-v1` at b978b63. The frozen v1 spec held 54 behaviours, 24 edges, 9 constraints and 14 interfaces mapped to 29 suites.

**[new] The roadmap.** After the problem hunt the branch wrote `dev/workspace/plans/flightcrew-features.md`, which is the most consequential document I found on this seam and the one my first report missed. It states ten features, one per registered problem, each written as the intention paragraph a spec would open with: F1 launch and run layout, F2 JSON document foundation, F3 runner as leaf tooling, F4 kickoff as workflow and arguments, F5 check rail and three verdicts, F6 roster and separations, F7 dispatch and return contracts, F8 enforcement that fails closed, F9 endings performed, F10 diagnosis reaches the next run. It then recommends a split into three launches in dependency order: **flightcrew-core** (F1, F2, F3, F6, F8), "the ground ... Nothing conducts a run yet; everything a conductor needs exists and holds"; **flightcrew-conduct** (F4, F7, F9, F10), "a run under its own power"; and **flightcrew-verdicts** (F5), "the check rail". Its closing rationale: "Launch 1 is the layer every later launch reads and writes ... Launch 2 is the intent's sentence and needs 1 underneath it. Launch 3 changes what a gate reads and is cheapest once gates are workflows. If one launch fails, the log entry names the axis and the next run of that launch differs by one change; the other two are untouched." Only launch 1 was ever opened as a spec. Launches 2 and 3 have no launch folder on any branch.

**Stalled or abandoned.** Launch `flightcrew-buildout` was abandoned at verify on a wrong-check escalation: two locked suite cases "cannot be satisfied by any faithful implementation", T11 expecting 2 added lines where git numstat reports 3, and the T24 import scanner reading a string literal as a specifier. A third finding was not a check: `.claude/workflows/flightcrew-build.js`, written by the driving session, sat outside the allowed paths and "needs a decision before the boundary can be clean". Launch `flightcrew-buildout-2` ended accepted-with-reservations at 19faef3. Its reservations, from RUNLOG: the tests map was revised to v2 by the orchestrator mid-build rather than after the human's log; the ending command was corrected during its own run; and five spec conflicts carried forward, namely B39 versus the workflow runtime (the shipped workflow scripts pass the suite but end with `export default`, which the runtime rejects), B27 worker render refusing the contracts phase the kickoff dispatches in, the I14 deny rule that would block the test-builder, the stall message on exit 0 that Claude Code does not display, and C3 one-line output versus validator lines. Six non-blocking observations F6 through F11 are open in `flightdeck/launch/flightcrew-buildout-2/report.md`. **[new]** The same report's earlier review pass records F1: locked suites (`bin-evidence/run.mjs`, `constraints/scan-lib.mjs`) and a locked spec-folder file were edited after `lock_commit`, breaking the verification and acceptance boundary guarantees. The "locked means unchanged" promise was broken by the system's own accepted run and legitimised after the fact by a mid-build re-pin rather than caught.

**[corrected]** My first report implied the orchestrator-pattern source guides were lost when 5b2d6ba deleted them. They are not. 8a56624 added HTML versions first and 5b2d6ba removed the now-redundant `.txt` originals; twelve HTML guides are present at branch head under `library/source/orchestrator-pattern/`, verified by `git ls-tree`. One explorer claimed the whole set was deleted and that claim is wrong.

**[new]** At 56bfb87 the core launch left this tree entirely. The commit body: "flightdeck/launch/flightcrew-core/ now lives on the flightcrew-core branch (cut from this commit's parent). This branch keeps the v1 system as built; the launch and its test-builder output stay in history here but leave the tree." So `flightcrew-buildout` at head is the v1 system as built, with no rework launch in it.

**Repaired or carried forward.** The buildout is the state every later branch tests or refactors. Its faults were catalogued deliberately: `a9389f8c` wrote seven problem documents to `dev/workspace/filebox/` covering structure, testing, fc, system, kickoff, crew and endings, and `bfb87cff` distilled those eight documents and 58 recorded defects into the ten spec-driving problems at `flightdeck/launch/flightcrew-core/specs/interview/problems.json`, which became the ten features above. **[new]** 6dcc00e raised the explorer's `maxTurns` from 12 to 48.

**Unfinished intent worth a mission.**
- **[new]** Launches 2 and 3 of the commander's own split, flightcrew-conduct and flightcrew-verdicts, were never opened. Evidence: `dev/workspace/plans/flightcrew-features.md`, Part 2. Why it matters: two epics already written at intention altitude, waiting only to be opened as specs.
- The central verdict of the problem hunt, from `a9389f8c`: "the builder invented a central fc runner nobody asked for that owns run state and is the human's control surface, and 57 of the spec's 78 behaviour nodes describe it." Why it matters: the single design fault the whole core refactor exists to undo, still in place.
- `a9389f8c` settled a target model never built: "freeze is enforced from the document's own status field and phase state lives in launch.json as an index a web page writes; a runner survives only as leaf commands behind pages and hooks". Why it matters: a designed-but-unbuilt architecture with a web page as the human's control surface.
- The five carried spec conflicts, above all B39. Why it matters: the workflow scripts ship green and do not run, which is why both later runs had to be conducted by hand.
- **[new]** The locked-path guarantee was violated by the accepted run itself (F1 above) and never re-established. Why it matters: F8, enforcement that fails closed, is a feature precisely because the system's own proof run broke its strongest promise.
- 1c81888 added `spec-altitude.md` and refined `spec-description.md`, the last commit on the branch, after every run had ended. It redefines interfaces as "common locations and languages ... built first so that the agent cannot invent its own version", reacting directly to v1's over-built command surface. Why it matters: doctrine written from the altitude lesson that has never been exercised by a run.
- **[new]** `flightdeck/missions/` on this branch holds one file, `ideas.keep`, reading "Ideas decomposed. Capture the Idea here." Why it matters: the missions layer that doctrine references was never populated, which is precisely the gap M001 exists to close.
- Two roles the constitution names are absent, and "dispatch is sealed for three roles and freehand for four" (`a9389f8c`). Why it matters: run 2 later pinned four crew defects confirming the roster is inconsistent by design.

## Seam 4 — `flightcrew-core` (+37, to 2026-09-13)

**Attempted.** Launch 1 of the three-launch split, driven by the ten problems. Per `9b679556`: "Settled: launch/<name>/{specs/,runs/run-<n>/} layout with launch.json as an index and no phase or gate state; configuration under flightdeck/.controlcenter/; freeze in the document; liftoffs as JSON recipes driving a workflow suite; a steward role runs scripts for workflows; a stalled worker gets one stronger attempt then the run stops; the ending after the report is the human's; the flight dispatcher is deferred." Nine judge passes and one attack, 44 findings absorbed. The spec holds 8 scope, 8 constraints, 12 interfaces, 35 behaviours, 14 edges and 9 decisions. Its SC1 is blunt: "the superseded v1 runner, hooks, kickoff library, dispatch templates, schemas ... and MANIFEST.txt are deleted." **[new]** The target runner is named `flight`, not `fc`.

**Stalled or abandoned.** Held, exactly as the commander says. The spec's `status` reads `"draft"`. It was frozen at 852b77c, then ef44303 reopened it, so as `9b679556` puts it: "the draft was frozen at 852b77c then reopened as draft after the altitude rewrite, so the freeze commit on the branch is void." **[new]** The ef44303 body is careful about this: "The freeze at 852b77c is superseded, not erased ... the flight dispatcher deferred in favour of core scripts by path. Questions logged in the register for later." `launch.json` still points `current_run: 1` at `base_commit` 7c5913e, the void freeze, with `tests_map: null` and `lock_commit: null`. The same summary states the decision to wait for a suite: "open a separate characterization launch whose product is a test suite at the human-observable boundary (labelled kept-deliberately or may-change), run it under the v1 tooling as the first real run, and cut flightcrew-core to the deltas (fc ripped out, workflows as conductor, launch/run layout, liftoffs, added roles, three verdicts) against that suite." No repair commit exists. An earlier v2 draft was abandoned outright before this v1 was written.

**Repaired or carried forward.** The rubric repair (93e18c5) landed here and is shared with buildout. The ten-problem register is the branch's durable asset. **[new]** 27f6969 and the opening of the characterization launch at 7f5ea3a share one session id and are minutes apart: the commander committed the void output and opened the characterization line in a single sitting.

**Unfinished intent worth a mission.**
- The whole repair. Per SC1 and the `9b679556` delta list. Why it matters: this is launch 1 of three and it has not started.
- **[corrected and expanded]** My first report named two spec contradictions. There are nine, all in `runs/run-1/returns/test-builder-map.json` under `spec_findings`, raised by the test-builder before any implementation: I8 (returns reused as they stand yet every return must carry target and fix), I13 (`phase` kept among check-result fields although D5 abolishes it), I9 (fix-dispatch path versus the render signature in I5), C13 (requiring a schema on every `agent()` call "whose return it branches on", which is not decidable from a script's text), I14 (a workflow denied the filesystem and the shell yet fc-build must cut waves), C2 (install writing under `.claude/` only with `--apply` versus I17 requiring the same command to write a line), B4 (`flight run new` creating a file set I1 names but which nothing writes), and I1 and SC1, which together report that "fc lint spec reports 71 lint-artefacts errors" from interfaces naming artefacts by bare filename and naming absolute paths that do not exist yet. Why it matters: the spec cannot be re-frozen until these are resolved, and 71 lint errors is a far larger repair than "two contradictions" suggested.
- Two nodes are unverified as unprovable, with the test-builder's own reasons. B70: the `{run}` token "is a property of a map a test-builder writes under the new runner, and this map must run under the current one, which does not expand `{run}`". E18: the scribe returning `assembled false` "requires dispatching the scribe against a launch with an input removed, which needs a model; the testbench has no probe harness and the conventions forbid a check that is not deterministic". Why it matters: both name exactly what must be built, a probe harness that dispatches one subagent against a fixture with a declared trials count and threshold.
- `flightdeck/.controlcenter/` is specified in SC2 and exists nowhere as working configuration. Why it matters: it is the seam the later web control surface plugs into.
- `9b679556` records the interview method lessons: "never invent mechanisms (run.json, fc-end, HALT.json, gates were all invented and removed); answer questions before acting; behaviours are outcomes with their reason, not dispatch mechanics". Why it matters: guardrails the buildout lacked, sitting in a transcript rather than in doctrine.
- No FLIGHTLOG entry exists for run 1. By that file's own rule a run is not opened until the previous entry carries no `<fill>` line. Why it matters: the ledger formally blocks the next run.

**The void run.** Run 1 was opened at 7c5913e and only ever dispatched a test-builder. Per `3b316953` it produced "a draft tests map ... with 51 checks and total coverage, baselined at commit 7c5913e with 48 fails, 3 world-state passes". **[corrected]** The commit body of 27f6969 gives the exact figures, and they are 47 suites, not the 49 I estimated: "The 47 suites, four library files and two fixtures the test-builder wrote against the void freeze (7c5913e), plus the hand-made FLIGHTLOG.md." **[new]** The same body states what these suites are for, which changes the picture materially: "They test the future system named by the core spec, so they live on this branch and not on flightcrew-buildout, whose run-all runs every suite it finds." The four libraries are `core-lib`, `orchestrator-rules`, `workflow-scan` and `workflow-stub`; the fixtures are `sample-launch-core` in the target layout and `sample-transcript` holding obedient, control and pull-request-failure orchestrator sessions. **[new]** I confirmed the run never ran: `runs/run-1/events.jsonl` and `hooks.log` are both zero bytes. The run is void because ef44303 superseded the freeze its checks were written against.

## Seam 5 — `flightcrew-characterization` and children

**Attempted.** A complete test suite over flightcrew as it stands, so the core refactor has something to lean on. The frozen spec's intent: "Flightcrew's test suite checks only its runner's commands. Nothing exercises a role, a hook or a workflow the way a working session does, because the manuals for testing agent-shaped work came after the build. Flightcrew is about to be refactored, and without a suite that describes it whole, every part the refactor touches is changed blind." SC4 forbids repair: a defect found is pinned, not fixed. SC5 excludes flightcrew-core; **[new]** SC9 excludes rubric calibration. Spec frozen at eca2bba (9 scope, 10 constraints, 4 interfaces, 12 behaviours, 10 edges, 8 decisions), tests map frozen at 266b10f with 61 checks. Two runs followed.

**Stalled or abandoned.** Run 1 was abandoned at implement (36f1536), and its RUNLOG entry is the most valuable single document in the repo. Symptom: "U0 (contracts, second dispatch) spent its 25-turn budget (ceilings.turns_per_agent 25) and stopped without delivering a worker return". Cause, stated flatly: "tooling. Ten faults, none in the spec or the map". The ten: no tool invokes a workflow, so the workflow shape is unusable; fc worker render refuses a contracts unit in phase contracts; the worktree guard refuses the fc shim, hot-patched mid-run at 85ed27e to `node fc.mjs`; three turn numbers exist and only frontmatter `maxTurns` binds, stopping U0 with no return; a stopped agent cannot be resumed and a fresh dispatch collides with the existing unit branch; explorers count against the agents ceiling; fc budget counts gate and human waits as run minutes, 1306 of them; no command changes a ceiling; escalation.json holds only the newest escalation; fc return has no `--stdin` while the orchestrator holds no Write. Plus two more: "fc check --baseline rewrote the frozen map's baseline commit, and fc launch pin's own instruction to allow the check commands put settings.json outside the boundary after the lock." b15f714 stored the run state immediately before the ending as the snapshot of what was lost.

**Repaired or carried forward.** Run 2 was fixed "by not using it". **[new]** Its kickoff says so directly: "Use custom workflows you write yourself ... No flightcrew runner, no hooks, no launch: nobody runs `flightdeck/flightcrew/bin/fc`." Checks are invoked by path, spec and map unchanged, same plan cut, all agents on opus, one worktree per unit with a verifier rerun and up to two repair rounds, then a critic pass over the final diff. It worked: 58 of 61 checks green at 4046d1e, `run-all.mjs` exit 0 with 33 suites, critic verdict pass with no blocking finding, six units U0 through U5 with only U2 refuted.

**What the suite characterizes.** Every part of flightcrew as it stands at the run's base. **[new]** The rewritten testbench README (b148c10) states the contract: a suite is a Node 22 ES module printing a fixed pass/FAIL/covers/count protocol; the part inventory is built from the runner's subcommands, the MANIFEST-listed hooks, roles, schemas, templates, validators, gates and workflow scripts, and everything `fc distribute` writes into `.claude/`; a sweep check breaks each part one specific way and needs at least one case that passes unaltered and fails altered; a defect is a part disagreeing with its own manual, header or usage, pinned via a `defect()` helper with a `<path>:<line>` reference that must resolve under a stated rule.

The three red checks are all check concerns, none fixable inside the allowed paths. T44 sweep-templates-1: `constitution-fragment.md` has no slot, key or version comment, so no alteration can turn a case red, and the human must choose between an exemption and a different alteration. T32 spec-copies: red at the base before any unit ran, because the three spec copies diverge from the frozen spec while `check-lib.mjs` hard-codes `SPEC_COMMIT ee8c088` and `FREEZE_COMMIT eca2bba`. T37 scope-untouched: also red at the base, because the run-2 setup commits changed four crew files, `worker-dispatch.template.md`, the `.claude/agents/flightcrew/` copies and `.claude/settings.json`.

The six pinned defects: explorer, verifier and critic carry no `maxTurns` although the manual implies every role does; the roster's turns of 12, 25, 15 and 20 do not match the files, with implementer at 200; spec-judge and spec-attacker carry no inputs line; the spec-builder body exceeds the 60-line cap; `fc launch gate G3 approve` records a gate decision on an already-ended launch instead of refusing it; and `validate-tests-map.mjs` reads `--spec` before the pinned spec beside the map, inverting its own documented precedence. Commit 3043f6c reverted a check because U2's second repair "pinned the heading against a requirement no manual states", the suite refusing to invent its own standard, which is the rule working.

**Unfinished intent worth a mission.**
- The run-2 result is stranded: `run/flightcrew-characterization-2` is 16 commits ahead of `flightcrew-characterization` and 39 ahead of `flightcrew-buildout`, never merged. Why it matters: the suite the core repair waits on exists and is not on the branch anyone would look at.
- No run-2 RUNLOG entry: "no `fc launch end` ran in a runner-free run". Why it matters: a reader of RUNLOG sees run 1's failure and not run 2's success, and the ledger rule blocks the next run.
- No scenario set was built under D3, so T16 through T21 pass empty. Why it matters: the judged and statistical classes are exactly what agent-shaped work needs, and they currently prove nothing while staying green.
- The reviewer's three acts on a fresh checkout and the transcript reading were never done. Why it matters: the run's acceptance is incomplete by its own definition.
- Four critic observations left open, F1 through F4, notably F3 where "a regression from writing to logging a failure stays green" and F2 where run-all charges only new `git status --porcelain` lines, so writes to already-modified, untracked or ignored paths go unseen. Why it matters: two are holes in the suite's own self-policing.
- 95a8cae on `flightcrew-characterization-1/contracts` is 31 files of protocol fixes inside U0's paths, checks unverified, "held for the human to salvage or delete". Why it matters: the only piece of run 1 with value, with an explicit pending decision on it.
- The RUNLOG `promote:` line names what must reach the core spec: "faults 1 to 10 above, above all that a conducting role must be able to invoke a workflow, that the harness turn limit is the only real budget and must leave a turn for the return, that waiting on a human is not run time, and that a frozen document must not be rewritten by a tool." Why it matters: a written, prioritised input to the core spec, and I found no evidence on any branch that the promotion ever happened.

## Reconciliation

**Buildout's two own commits past 1b0a8ba** are 56bfb87 (2026-09-13) "Move the flightcrew-core launch to its own branch" and 1c81888 (2026-09-15) "Add spec-altitude.md and refine spec-description.md". The first cut the core launch out of the tree so the branch could stand as the v1 system as built; the second is doctrine written after the core interview taught the altitude lesson.

**Core's one own commit past 1b0a8ba** is 27f6969 (2026-09-13), the void run's test-builder output: 47 suites, four libraries, two fixtures and a hand-made FLIGHTLOG.

**The characterization suite tests the buildout state and carries no core changes.** The merge-base of `flightcrew-core` and `flightcrew-characterization` is 1b0a8ba, so 27f6969 is not in it. Characterization forks from buildout at 56bfb87 and so also lacks 1c81888. SC5 says so: "the flightcrew-core spec, its branch, its layout, workflows, liftoffs and roles are not read and not tested."

**[corrected] The two testbench trees are not duplicates, they are before and after.** My first report treated core's 47 suites and run 2's 33 as rival versions of the same thing. The 27f6969 commit body corrects that: core's suites "test the future system named by the core spec", while characterization's test flightcrew exactly as it stands. The collision is real but different in kind, and the commit body names the mechanism: they were segregated from buildout precisely because its "run-all runs every suite it finds". Characterization's run-all behaves the same way, so merging the two trees makes run-all discover 47 suites for a system that does not exist yet and fail. Sequencing, not conflict resolution, is what this needs.

**Pure ancestors, safe to forget as branches.** `flightcrew-buildout-v1` (merged as PR #1 at b978b63), `run/flightcrew-core-1`, `run/flightcrew-characterization-1`, all six `worktree-wf_*` branches, and `flightcrew-characterization-2/contracts` and `flightcrew-characterization-2/roles-workflows-distributed`. I confirmed each is contained in its successor; the two unit branches were folded in by the wave-merge commits 5287889, 7e74ace, 26386fc and 845e6f5.

**Not debris, keep.** `flightcrew-characterization-1/contracts` is one commit past its merge-base and is the salvage-or-delete decision named in RUNLOG. `run/flightcrew-characterization-2` is 16 commits of unmerged finished work and is the most valuable unlanded ref in the repo.

**Stale remotes.** `origin/flightcrew-buildout` is at 93e18c5, eleven behind local. `origin/flightcrew-characterization` is at 56ca431, missing run 2 entirely. `origin/main` is two behind. A local branch literally named `origin` at 1f20b49 is migration debris.

The commander's own three-launch split is the right organising frame, and missions 3, 6 and 7 below are simply its launches. The rest are what must happen before launch 1 can open.

# mission-cutter: epic missions — M001

## Campaign
The campaign already has an organising frame and the commander wrote it: `dev/workspace/plans/flightcrew-features.md` states ten features and splits them into three launches in dependency order, flightcrew-core (the ground), flightcrew-conduct (a run under its own power) and flightcrew-verdicts (the check rail). Only launch 1 was ever opened as a spec, and it is held. So the spine is six missions: land the finished characterization suite so the system has a truthful description of itself (M002); make the harness the system runs on truthful, because both surviving runs were conducted by hand around broken tooling (M003); repair and re-freeze the core spec, which is nine contradictions and 71 lint errors rather than the two it looked like (M004); then fly the commander's three launches in their own order (M005, M006, M007). M002 gates M004 because the core spec's held state exists precisely to wait for a suite. M003 gates M005 because a refactor conducted by hand leaves no evidence trail, and because several planned fixes rest on claims about Claude Code that have never been tested. Beside the spine run the commander's two cockpit missions, which start immediately and never block: mine the dispatch record into reusable teams (M008), and mine the commander's decisions into standing doctrine (M009). M009 first among equals, because its product is the alignment input every later brief reads.

## The eight
- **M002 Land the characterization suite and sequence the two testbench trees.** Merge `run/flightcrew-characterization-2`, write the run-2 ledger entry, decide T32, T37 and T44, and keep core's 47 future-system suites from running against the present system. Carries the `1c81888` spec-altitude doctrine seed, since characterization forked at `56bfb87` and never received it. First launch: a merge analyst producing a sequencing plan and a decision sheet, no branch touched.
- **M003 Make the harness truthful.** Verdict on all seventeen harness claims, direct tests for the four unsupported ones, a conducting role demonstrably invoking a workflow, one binding turn number, human waits out of run minutes, `omitClaudeMd: true` on sealed roles, locked-path guarantee restored. Sweeps in G3 having no code behind it. First launch: a verification crew returning a verdict sheet before anything is built.
- **M004 Repair and re-freeze the core spec.** Nine `spec_findings` resolved, 71 lint-artefacts errors cleared, both `promote:` lines traceable to spec nodes, B70 and E18 decided, freeze real rather than void. First launch: a spec-attacker pass briefed with the promote lines.
- **M005 Fly launch 1, flightcrew-core: the ground.** F1, F2, F3, F6, F8. The invented runner deleted, freeze in the document, `.controlcenter/` real, roster consistent, enforcement fails closed. First launch: a delta mapper, not an implementer.
- **M006 Fly launch 2, flightcrew-conduct: a run under its own power.** F4, F7, F9, F10. Absorbs the conducting-role and budget-model fixes durably. First launch: an explorer pass mapping every human intervention in runs 1 and 2.

First launch: a manifest miner returning composition and failure tables with no recommendation.
- **M009 Mine the commander's decisions into standing doctrine (commander-stated).** Every session logged, standing orders near-verbatim and dated, one mined order ratified, contradictions surfaced, mining done by a team. First launch: a log miner returning candidate orders with source lines.

<teammate-message teammate_id="source-reader" color="pink" summary="Addendum filed — both documents read, branch check done">
# source-reader addendum — T002

**agentic-principles.md** (`constitution-research:library/constitution/agentic-principles.md`). Purpose: the foundation layer of universal, HITL (human-in-the-loop) agentic-coding principles that the other two documents restate or build on. Eight principles, one line each, all citing the same four collective sources (no per-principle citation in the text):
- Context engineering — smallest high-signal material, state the perimeter not just the target.
- Verification — a check the agent can read beats "looks done".
- Observability — ask intention, watch direction, steer at the first sign of drift.
- Review — isolated judgement, diff and criteria only, nothing of the reasoning.
- Decomposition — split by size or by kind; explore/plan/implement/commit apart.
- Isolation and recoverability — worktrees, small frequent commits, git as the only durable record.
- Budgets and stop conditions — match model to task, restart rather than rescue.
- Permissions and blast radius — minimum grant, deny rules for the irreversible.
Sources (collective, all four cited at document end): https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents, https://www.anthropic.com/research/building-effective-agents, https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/writing-tools-for-agents, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices.

**orchestration-tooling.md** (`constitution-research:library/constitution/orchestration-tooling.md`). Purpose: not a principles document — it enumerates the tools, architecture and infrastructure a run needs to exist before liftoff, one section per requirement (spec, agent roles, frontmatter permissions, CLAUDE.md, skills, hooks, test harness, goal evaluator, liftoff prompt, plan, dynamic workflows, worktrees/commits/branches, handoffs, run outputs, final review, run log), each stating the human function it replaces and the harness feature that implements it. It has no "principles" in the same sense as the other two; its "Agent Invariants" section (six bullets) is the closest analogue. Sources, eight listed at the end, all Anthropic: best-practices, workflows, goal, sub-agents, hooks, worktrees, effective-context-engineering, writing-tools-for-agents — same URLs as orchestration-principles.md's list, no distinct citations of its own.

**Relationship to orchestration-principles.md.** Stated textually, not inferred. `agentic-principles.md` line 5: "Other documents restate the same principles under different operating conditions. This one is the foundation those restatements draw from, and the definitions here are the ones they refer back to." `orchestration-principles.md` line 5 confirms from the other side: "The first half of this document takes the universal agentic principles in their original order and restates each for a run." So `orchestration-principles.md` derives from `agentic-principles.md` — same eight principles, reworded for a run, plus six new run-only ones with no HITL counterpart. No conflict between them; orchestration-principles is agentic-principles specialised for unattended execution. `orchestration-tooling.md` does not cross-reference either document and is not a restatement of either — it's a companion inventory (what must exist) alongside two companions that state how to behave (principles) — no overlap in content, no conflict, different genre entirely.

**Reached any later branch?** No. `git ls-tree -r --name-only <branch> | grep -iE "principles|agentic|tooling"` on `main`, `flightcrew-buildout` and `run/flightcrew-characterization-2` finds nothing for `agentic-principles.md` or `orchestration-tooling.md` under any filename on any of the three. `main` carries only `dev/workspace/filebox/orchestration-principles-review.md` and `orchestration-principles.backup.md` (the review and a backup of the principles document itself, already known from T001) — no trace of either of the two documents read here. All three constitution documents are confined to `constitution-research`.

**F1** — **The identity quad is stored four times and three copies are already stale**
**S-H** · **C-H**
**FINDING**: `name`, `agent_type`, `model`, `dossier` appear in a `cockpit.json` seat, a `rosters.json` seat, a `crew.json` row and a dossier header. Make `crew.json` the sole source; a `cockpit.json` seat becomes `{name, reason, returns}` and a `rosters.json` seat becomes a bare name string.

**EVIDENCE**: I tested all 25 rows against `f"{room}/{name}.md"`. Zero exceptions. 25 lines that can only ever go wrong.

Carry the one operative sentence in the `crew.json` `status` string, which already says `retired into fit-critic`, `retired into docs-verifier`, `retired into adversary`, `superseded by record-writer`.
**EVIDENCE**: 100 lines / 481 words across the five. `team/red-team.md` runs four headed sections to deliver `Superseded by the adversary seat on Opus`; its one substantive point, that Sonnet is wrong on an adversary seat, is already CLAUDE.md crew rule 2.
**LOST**: `records-writer.md` is the substantial one at 172 words, but its lessons are already in T002's `lessons` array in `cockpit.json`. Net loss across the five is nothing not held elsewhere.

**F7** — **`cockpit.json` seat `reads` restates the dossier's brief essentials**
**S-M** · **C-M**
**FINDING**: Drop `reads` from the seat entry. Keep `reason` and `returns`, which are the team-specific facts nothing else records.
**EVIDENCE**: 149 words over 26 seats. T001's `docs-verifier` reads `official Anthropic docs`; `team/docs-verifier.md` states the same scope durably. Saving: 26 lines.
**LOST**: the exact per-team scope, where a seat's scope differed from its standing one. `reason` usually implies it, and `returns` shows what the scope produced.

**F8** — **The in-file `fields` and `purpose` blocks restate the sibling README**
**S-M** · **C-M**
**FINDING**: Delete the `fields` block from `cockpit.json` (10 lines) and from `crew.json` (9 lines), and cut the paragraph-length `purpose` strings in `crew.json` and `rosters.json` to one clause each. Keep the READMEs as the description of record; keep the `fields` line for any convention a reader cannot infer, such as F2's filename rule.
**EVIDENCE**: `cockpit.json` `fields` says the seat carries `name, agent_type, model, dossier (quarters/crew/<room>/<file>), reason, reads, returns`; `dispatch/README.md` bullet one says `one entry per team with its seats inside (name, agent type, model, dossier, reason, reads, returns)`. `crew.json` `purpose` restates the whole of `quarters/crew/README.md` in 62 words. The files sit in the same directory as their README.
**LOST**: the JSON stops being self-describing when read alone. It is never read alone; the README is one directory listing away.

**F9** — **`dispatch/README.md` documents a file that does not exist**
**S-L** · **C-H**
**FINDING**: Cut the `flightcrew.json` bullet to a half-line, or to nothing until launches start.
**EVIDENCE**: `ls dispatch/` returns `cockpit.json`, `rosters.json`, `README.md`. The bullet spends a line and a parenthetical on departments that are not built.
**LOST**: the signal that the room is designed to take a second file. The README's opening sentence already frames the room by room.

**F10** — **Accounting for what I am not cutting**
**S-L** · **C-H**
**FINDING**: `cockpit.json` `outcome` and `lessons` total 798 words and are the largest prose in the three JSONs. I attack nothing in them.
**EVIDENCE**: They are the only record of what a team actually did, they load once a session rather than per dispatch, and F5 and F6 both spend them as the place the deleted history survives. Cutting them would break the other cuts.
**LOST**: would be the integration's reason to exist.

---

**The three cuts that save most at zero functional cost.**

1. **F1, one source for seat identity.** `crew.json` owns name, agent type, model and dossier; `cockpit.json` and `rosters.json` hold names and pointers. Removes 175 lines and the drift that has already happened three times.
2. **F2 with F3, delete the derived fields.** `dossier`, `teams` and `flown` are all computable. Removes 110 lines from `crew.json` and 16 from `rosters.json`, and leaves nothing that can be wrong except the one legacy string.
3. **F4 with F5, put the dossiers on a diet.** Header cut to name and purpose, history merged into one 40-word record. Takes dossiers from 3705 to about 2400 words on the file class that loads every time a seat is dispatched.

Together: the three JSONs go 857 → 536 lines, a 37% cut; the dossiers go 3705 → about 2400 words, a 35% cut; five files disappear. No fact leaves the room.

**Verdict.** The integration is sound in shape and overweight in storage, and the overweight is one mistake repeated: the same four facts written into four files by hand, with no derivation and no check. That is not a style complaint, it is a defect that has already fired — T008 was filed correctly in `cockpit.json` and is wrong in three other places as I write this, hours after the room was built. Every cut above follows from naming one owner per fact: `crew.json` owns who a seat is, `cockpit.json` owns what a team did, the dossier owns how to brief the seat next time, the README owns the explanation. The dossiers are the second problem and the more expensive one per session, because they load on dispatch and spend a third of their words telling a future pilot a story instead of an instruction. Do F1 through F5 and the room holds the same knowledge in about two thirds of the bytes, with one place to write each fact.

Standing by for one round of questions.
